General Horizontal Norm Compatible Systems Thanks: The author was supported by the Swiss National Science Foundation grant #PP00P2-144658 and #P2ELP2-191672.
Reda Boumasmoud

Abstract
. The ultimate goal of the paper is to construct a novel norm-compatible family of cycles appearing in the context of Gross–Gan–Prasad cycles [ GGP09 ] arising from Shimura varieties attached to U ⁡ ( n − 1 , 1 ) ↪ U ⁡ ( n , 1 ) × U ⁡ ( n − 1 , 1 ) U(n-1,1)\hookrightarrow U(n,1)\times U(n-1,1) for arbitrary n n .



1.
Introduction

Arithmetic geometry could be defined as the study of motives over number fields. It is organized around a handful of conjectures.
One of them is due to Beilinson, Bloch and Kato and it relates the special values of the
L
-functions attached to algebraic varieties or, more generally, motives to the arithmetic invariants of these algebraic objects, such as their Bloch–Kato Selmer groups and the regulators arising from them.
This conjecture can be interpreted as a remarkable attempt to unify a few conjectures that have been wandering in number theory’s paysage for a few decades, such as the Birch–Swinnerton–Dyer conjecture and the main conjecture of Iwasawa theory.

Euler systems are currently one of the most efficient and fruitful
1
1

1

There is also the method of Ribet(–Mazur–Wiles), generalized to the
\GL_{2}
-setting by Skinner and Urban and the method of Wiles (
"R=T"
) that yields results for
L
-functions adjoint motives.
tool to approach cases of Bloch–Kato type conjectures. They consist of ”geometric” families of classes in the continuous Galois cohomology groups containing the relevant Bloch–Kato Selmer groups, which are related via ”explicit reciprocity laws” to the special values one wishes to study: If
E
is a number field and
T
a
p
-representation of
\text{Gal}(\overline{E}/E)
,
an Euler system
2
2

2

I would like to avoid here the difficult task of defining what an ”Euler/Kolyvagin” system is.
over
T
is a collection of (Galois) cohomology classes in
\mathbf{H}^{1}(L,T):=\mathbf{H}^{1}(\text{Gal}(\overline{L}/L),T)
, indexed by finite abelian fields extension
E\subset L\subset E[\infty]
, for some fixed infinite abelian extension
E[\infty]
.
These classes are constrained by a set of compatibility conditions for norm application
\mathbf{H}^{1}(L,T)\to\mathbf{H}^{1}(E,T)
involving appropriate Euler factors between them.
In general, the existence of a non-trivial Euler System adds just enough ”rigidity” on the relevant Bloch–Selmer groups to capture its structure.

There are three major types norm-compatibility relations: horizontal (or tame), vertical and congruence relations.
Horizontal norm relations aim to model the local L-factor of some L-function
(e.g., the L-function of a modular form or an elliptic curve) using cohomological data.
Vertical norm relations
refer to compatibility of cohomology classes in
\mathds{Z}_{p}
-extensions of
E
.
They are key ingredient in the setting of Iwasawa theory; they yield upper bounds for the size of Selmer groups of
T
(by a theorem of Rubin which generalizes the work of Kolyvagin) and so plays an fundamental part for the proven case of the Iwasawa main conjecture.

Assume that
T
is a group of
p
-adic cohomology étale
\mathbf{H}^{r-1}(X_{\overline{E}},\mathds{Q}_{p}(m))
, where
X
is an algebraic variety over
E
and
m\geq r/2
. One approach to obtain Euler systems is by means of the Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence

E_{2}^{pq}\to\mathbf{H}^{p}(E,\mathbf{H}^{q}(X_{\overline{E}},\mathds{Q}_{p}(m)))\Rightarrow\mathbf{H}^{p+q}(X,\mathds{Q}_{p}(m))

which links the étale cohomolgy of
X_{\overline{E}}
with Jannsen’ continuous cohomology group
\mathbf{H}^{r}(X,\mathds{Q}_{p}(m))
.
First, we construct classes in the cohomology
\mathbf{H}^{r-1}(X,\mathds{Q}_{p}(m))
3
3

3

For example, if
m=r/2
, classes of algebraic cycles over
X
that are defined over abelian extensions of
E
and of correct dimension
. If these classes are in the kernel
\mathcal{F}^{1}
of the boundary homomorphism

\mathbf{H}^{r}(X,\mathds{Q}_{p}(m))\to\mathbf{H}^{r}(\overline{X},\mathds{Q}_{p}(m))
, then one can take their images by the differential
\mathcal{F}^{1}\to\mathbf{H}^{1}(E,\mathbf{H}^{r-1}(X_{\overline{E}},T))
and the verification of the compatibility conditions on these classes is reduced to similar compatibilities for their corresponding algebraic cycles.

Whereas it is very hard to construct and control algebraic cycles of a general variety, the situation is much more pleasant for Shimura varieties. In this case, we have in hands an abundant source of malleable cycles, these are the special cycles obtained from Hecke translates of connected component of Shimura subvarieties of smaller dimensions.

A fundamental historical example is Kolyvagin’s Heegner points Euler system, which consist of the family of classes in the cohomology of elliptic curves arising from Heegner points. This construction arises from the embeddings of the tori associated to an imaginary quadratic field
\mathcal{K}
into forms of the group
\GL_{2}
and generates a collection of points on modular (or Shimura) curves, which are defined over ring class fields of
\mathcal{K}
and which can be mapped to elliptic curves via modular parametrizations.
Kolyvagin ’s pioneer method exploited the horizontal relations of this Euler system to give, combined with the Gross--Zagier formula
4
4

4

and some analytic results due to Murty–Murty and Bump–Friedberg–Hoffstein

[
BFH90
,
MM97
]

[
Kol90
,
GZ86
]
, the strongest evidence towards the Birch and Swinerton-Dyer conjecture.

Classical Euler systems have been fairly thoroughly exploited already in late 1980’s and in 1990’s.
Unfortunately, the list of constructed ones was rather short
[
Kol90
,
Kat04
]
.
But in recent years, there has been a surge of new candidates proposed by various people (for instance; Cornut
[
Cor18
]
, Jetchev
[
Jet16
]
, Boumasmoud–Brooks–Jetchev
[
BBJ18
,
BBJ16
]
, Loeffler–Zerbes–Lei
[
LLZ14
,
LLZ17
]
, Loeffler–Zerbes–Kings
[
KLZ15
,
KLZ17
]
and, Loeffler–Zerbes–Skinner
[
LSZ17
]
). Judging by the success of their
predecessors, one expects that these new Euler Systems should soon yield significant new developments in arithmetic geometry.

Establishing norm compatibility relations for the early Euler systems constructed out of Heegner points or Siegel units was a pleasant exercise in CM theory and modular functions.
Yet, the situation changed dramatically for the new applicants to the point that most of the difficulty for obtaining constructions coming from subvarieties of higher-dimensional Shimura varieties seems to be now concentrated in this new bottleneck.
The sought for relations should involve the Euler factor that was first defined by Langlands, namely, the Hecke polynomial attached to the minuscule cocharacter appearing in the definition of the ambient Shimura variety.
Gradually, this has become an underground subfield of its own, with a handful of experts with strong connections and shared preoccupations with the larger community of people working in
p
-adic L-functions, starving for new methods that would yield easier - or at least more conceptual - proofs of these relations.
Such methods would be especially welcome in the lack of general strategy to establish the desired distribution relations for a given collection of special cycles on a Shimura variety.
This paper attempts to contribute to this part of the theory by proposing a general construction (using
\mathbb{U}
-operators) which emerges from the proof of the main theorem as explained in §
1.2
.

The ultimate goal of the paper is to construct a novel norm-compatible family of cycles appearing in the context of Gross–Gan–Prasad cycles
[
GGP09
]
arising from Shimura varieties attached to
U(n-1,1)\hookrightarrow U(n,1)\times U(n-1,1)
for arbitrary
n
. These cycles were first considered by Jetchev
[
Jet16
]
in the simplest non-classical case
n=2
and further developed by Boumasmoud, Brooks and Jetchev in
[
BBJ16
]
and
[
BBJ18
]
.

1.1.
From classical Heegner points towards a general construction

In order to outline the strategy of the proof, let us first briefly review the classical case of heegner points on a modular curve.

Let
N
be an integer and
E/\mathds{Q}
be an imaginary quadratic field with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{E}
. Assume that all primes of
N
split in
E
and let
\mathcal{N}
be an ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
of norm
N
. If
m
is prime to
N
, the isogeny
\mathds{C}/\mathcal{O}_{m}\to\mathds{C}/(\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{O}_{m})^{-1}
corresponds to a Heegner point
x_{m}
in
X_{0}(N)(E[m])
, where
E[m]
denotes the ring class field of conductor
m
and
\mathcal{O}_{m}=\mathds{Z}+m\mathcal{O}_{E}
is the corresponding order of
E
. Set
\text{CM}_{E}:=\{x_{m}\colon m\text{ prime to }N\}
. The points in
\text{CM}_{E}
are related by the following norm-compatibilities
[
Dar04
, Proposition 3.10]
:

Proposition 1.1.1

(
Distribution relations
)
.

Let
m
be an integer and
\ell
a prime which is unramified over
E
. We also suppose that
m\ell
is prime to
N
. In this case, we have

(i)

Tame relations:
Let
\lambda
be a prime of
E
that lies over
\ell
. If
\ell\nmid m
, then

(1.1)

\Tr_{E[m\ell]/E[m]}x_{m\ell}=\left(T_{\ell}-\Fr_{\lambda}-\left(\frac{\mathfrak{d}_{E}}{\ell}\right)\Fr_{\lambda}^{-1}\right)x_{m},

where,
T_{\ell}
denotes the Hecke operator corresponding to
[\GL_{2}(\mathds{Z}_{\ell})\text{diag}(\ell,1)\GL_{2}(\mathds{Z}_{\ell})]
and
\Fr_{\lambda}\in\text{Gal}(E[\infty]/E)
denotes the geomteric Frobenius.

(ii)

Vertical relations:
if
\ell\mid m
, then

(1.2)

\Tr_{E[m\ell]/E[m]}x_{m\ell}=T_{\ell}x_{m}-x_{m/\ell}.

Now, consider the Hecke polynomial

H_{\ell}(X)=X^{2}-T_{\ell}X+\ell S_{\ell},

where,
S_{\ell}
denotes the Hecke operator corresponding to the double coset
[\GL_{2}(\mathds{Z}_{\ell})\text{diag}(\ell,\ell)\GL_{2}(\mathds{Z}_{\ell})]
.
There exists an operator
\mathcal{U}_{\ell}\in\text{End}_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[\text{CM}_{E}]
(see Figure
1
and
[
Bou21c
, Introduction]
), a variant of the combinatorial ”successor” operator
5
5

5

It may be compared with Cornut–Vatsal operator
T_{P}^{u}
in
[
CV07
, 6.3]
.
, verifying the properties below:

i.

The Hecke side:

(1.3)

H_{\ell}(\mathcal{U}_{\ell})=0\text{ in }\text{End}_{\mathds{Z}}\mathds{Z}[\text{CM}_{E}].

ii.

The Galois side:
Let
\ell\nmid m
. For
s\geq 1
, we have

(1.4)

\Tr_{E[m\ell^{s+1}]/E[m\ell^{s}]}x_{m\ell^{s+1}}=\mathcal{U}_{\ell}x_{m\ell^{s}},

iii.

The Congruence side:
If
\ell\nmid m
, then

(1.5)

(\mathcal{U}_{\ell}-\ell\Fr_{\lambda})x_{m}\equiv 0\mod\left(\ell-\left(\frac{\mathfrak{d}_{E}}{\ell}\right)\right)\text{ in }\mathds{Z}[\mathcal{O}_{E_{\ell}}^{\times}\backslash\text{CM}_{E}],

where
\mathfrak{d}_{E}
denotes the different ideal of
E
.

It is an easy exercise to verify (see

1.2.
A general recipe for horizontal/vertical norm relations families

A strategy to construct norm compatible families (for general embeddings of Shimura data) emerges from the the reformulation of §
1.1
and it is confirmed by the method of proof of Theorem
1.3.2
. It goes as follows:

(1)

The Hecke side:
In
[
Bou21c
]
, we define and study the arithmetic of
\mathbb{U}
-operators in terms of Iwahori–Hecke algebras. This is done in utmost generality, for arbitrary connected reductive groups over p-adic fields.
In the unramified case, we prove that the
\mathbb{U}
-operators attached to a cocharacter (not necessarily minuscule) is a right root of the corresponding Hecke polynomial
[
Bou21b
, Theorem
]
.

(2)

The congruence side:
As the method of proof involves only the
\mathbb{U}
-operator and its powers through Lemma
4.5.3
; the Hecke polynomial enters the picture only through
1
.

(3)

The Galois side:
This is dealt with in
[
Bou21a
]
, it generalizes the equation (
1.4
) for the general unitary case and gives evidence how
{U}
-operators can provide a strategy to establish vertical norm relations involving other Shimura varieties and also vertical norm relations.

A much more detailed description of this recipe for constructing (horizontal) normal compatible systems of cycles of Shimura varieties will be the subject of another upcoming paper.

1.3.
Main results of the paper

1.3.1.
The Gan–Gross–Prasad setting

Gancmcm Gross and Prasad formulated some conjectures
6
6

6

These conjectures may be thought of as Gross–Zagier-type formulas the cycles being generalizations of classical Heegner points.
relating special values of derivatives of automorphic
L
-functions to heights of certain special cycles on Shimura varieties constructed from embeddings of reductive groupscmcm e.g.
[
GGP09
, Conjecture 27.1]
. In this papercmcm we consider the case of special cycles on higher-dimensional Shimura varietiescmcm where the embedding

\Res_{E/\mathds{Q}}\mathbf{G}_{m,E}\hookrightarrow\GL_{2,\mathds{Q}}
defining Heegner points is replaced by an embedding of unitary groups
\mathbf{U}(n-1cmcm1)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{U}(ncmcm1)\times\mathbf{U}(n-1cmcm1)
associated to a CM-extension
E/F
.

(§
2.1
) Let
E
be a CM fieldcmcm that iscmcm an imaginary quadratic extension of a totally real number field
F
. Set
[E:\mathds{Q}]=2[F:\mathds{Q}]=2d
. Let
\tau
be the non-trivial element of
\text{Gal}(E/F)
. Fix an integer
n>1
. Let
W
be a Hermitian
E
-space of dimension
n
and of signature
(n-1cmcm1)
at one fixed distinguished embedding
\iota\colon E\hookrightarrow\mathds{C}
and, of signature
(n,0)
at the other archimedean places. Let
D
be a positive definite Hermitian
E
-line. Consider the
n+1
-dimensional Hermitian
E
-space
V=W\oplus D
, it has signature
(n,1)
at the distinguished archimedean place and, signature
(n,0)
at the other ones.

We consider the
F
-algebraic reductive groups of unitary isometries
\mathbf{U}(V)
and
\mathbf{U}(W)
. Set
\mathbf{G}_{V}:=\Res_{F/\mathds{Q}}\mathbf{U}(V)
and
\mathbf{G}_{W}:=\Res_{F/\mathds{Q}}\mathbf{U}(W)
. We identify
\mathbf{G}_{W}
with the subgroup of
\mathbf{G}_{V}
. Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}_{V}\times\mathbf{G}_{W}
and

\mathbf{H}=\Delta(\mathbf{G}_{W})\subset\mathbf{G}
, where
\Delta
denotes the diagonal embedding
\Delta\colon\mathbf{G}_{W}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
.

(§
2.3
) Let
\mathcal{X}_{V}
be the Hermitian symmetric domain consisting of negative definite lines in
V\otimes_{F,\iota}\mathds{R}
and similarly let
\mathcal{X}_{W}
be the set of negative definite lines in
W\otimes_{F,\iota}\mathds{R}
. Setting
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{V}\times\mathcal{X}_{W}
, the diagonal embedding
W\hookrightarrow V\oplus W
induces an embedding of Hermitian symmetric domains
\mathcal{X}_{W}
into
\mathcal{X}
; set
\mathcal{Y}
for the image of
\mathcal{X}_{W}
.

(§
2.6
) The two pairs
(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
and
({\Hbf},\mathcal{Y})
are Shimura data. For small enough compact open subgroup
K_{\mathbf{G}}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
(resp.
K_{\Hbf}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
), the Shimura variety
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
(resp.
\Sh_{K_{\Hbf}}({\Hbf},\mathcal{Y})
) is a complex quasi-projective smooth variety whose
\mathds{C}
-points are given by

\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})\backslash(\mathcal{X}\times(\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\penalty K_{\mathbf{G}}))\quad(\text{resp. }{\Hbf}(\mathds{Q})\backslash(\mathcal{Y}\times({\Hbf}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\penalty K_{\Hbf}))),

where
\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})
(resp.
{\Hbf}(\mathds{Q})
) acts diagonally on
\mathcal{X}\times(\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\penalty K_{\mathbf{G}})
(resp.
\mathcal{Y}\times({\Hbf}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\penalty K_{\Hbf}))
. In fact, these varieties are defined over the reflex field
E=E(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})=E({\Hbf},\mathcal{Y})
(See §
2.4
for the calculation of the reflex field).

(§
3.1
) For every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, we will denote by
\mathfrak{z}_{g}
the
n
-codimensional
{\Hbf}
-special cycle
[\mathcal{Y}\times gK]\subset\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C})
, as defined in Definition
3.1.2
. Set,

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}({\Hbf}):=\{\mathfrak{z}_{g}:g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f}).\}

The natural map
\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})\to\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}({\Hbf})
given by
g\mapsto\mathfrak{z}_{g}
, induces the bijection

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}({\Hbf})\simeq{\Hbf}(\mathds{Q}){Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K.

(§
3.2
) The
{\Hbf}
-special cycles
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}({\Hbf})
are all defined over the transfer class field
E(\infty)
(§
3.3
).

(§
3.4
) The Galois group
\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E)
acts on the set of special cycles
through
the left action of
H(\mathds{A}_{f})
. More precisely, for every
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E)
, we let
h_{\sigma}\in{\Hbf}(\mathds{A}_{f})
be any element verifying
Art_{E}^{1}(\det(h_{\sigma})\cdot\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})=\sigma|_{E(\infty)}
. For every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, we have

\sigma(\mathfrak{z}_{g})=\mathfrak{z}_{h_{\sigma}g}.

(§
3.6
) For
\star\in\{W,V\}
, we fix any compact open subgroups
K_{\star}\subset\mathbf{U}_{\star}(\mathds{A}_{F,f})
. There exists a finite set
S
of places of
F
such that
K_{\star}
is of the form
K_{\star,S}\times K_{\star}^{S}
where
K_{\star,S}
is some compact open subgroup of
\mathbf{U}_{\star}(\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{S})
and
K_{\star}^{S}
is the product of the hyperspecial compact open subgroups
K_{\star,v}:=\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{\star}(\mathcal{O}_{F_{v}})\subset\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{\star}(F_{v})
for all
v\not\in S
. In particular,
K_{W,v}=K_{V,v}\cap\underline{\mathbf{U}}_{W}(F_{v})
. Set
K_{v}:=K_{V,v}\times K_{W,v}
.

1.3.2.
Main theorems on distribution

Set

\mathcal{P}_{sp}:=\left\{\mathfrak{p}\in\text{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{F})\colon\mathfrak{p}\text{ is split in }E/F,\mathfrak{p}\not\in S,\mathfrak{p}\nmid\mathfrak{c}_{1},\mathfrak{p}\mathcal{O}_{E}\nmid I_{0}\right\},

where
S
is defined in §
3.6.4
,
\mathfrak{c}_{1}
in Remark
3.9.2
and
I_{0}
in Lemma
3.9.5
. Denote by
\mathcal{N}_{sp}
the set of square free products of primes in
\mathcal{P}_{sp}
. For every place
v
in
\mathcal{P}_{sp}
corresponding to the prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{v}\in\mathcal{N}_{sp}
, let
w
be the place of
E
defined by the embedding
\iota_{v}\colon\overline{F}\hookrightarrow\overline{F}_{v}
fixed in §
2
. We denote by
{\mathfrak{P}_{w}}
the prime ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
above
\mathfrak{p}_{v}
corresponding to the place
w
and set
\Fr_{w}
for the corresponding
geometric
Frobenius
7
7

7

Which induces
x\mapsto x^{-q_{v}}
on the residue fields of
E_{v}^{un}
(resp.
E_{w}^{un}
and
E_{\overline{w}}^{un}
).
. Let
\textbf{Frob}_{w}\in\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})
be any element such that
\Art_{E}^{1}(\textbf{Frob}_{w})|_{E(\infty)^{un,w}}=\Fr_{w}
, where
E(\infty)^{un,w}
is the maximal unramified at
w
extension in
E(\infty)
.

Local congruence relations.
As we have pointed out in the Heegner case, the horizontal norm compatibility relation below are derived from local congruence relations (see Lemma
4.5.3
and Theorem
4.5.2
) generalizing the congruence equality (
1.5
):

Theorem 1.3.1
.

With the above notation, we have

H_{w}(\textbf{Frob}_{w})([1]_{v})\equiv 0\mod q_{v}^{n-1}\left(q_{v}-1\right)\quad\text{in }\mathds{Z}[q_{v}^{-1}][{\mathbf{H}}^{\der}(F_{v})\backslash{\mathbf{G}}(F_{v})/K_{v}],

where
H_{w}
is the Hecke polynomial attached to
\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
at the place
{w}
of the reflex field
E=E(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
.

Tame relations.
As a corollary of Theorem
1.3.1
, we obtain local horizontal relations in Corollary
4.5.4
, from which we derive the tame relations.

In §
3.6.4
, we fix a cycle
\xi_{1}:=\mathfrak{z}_{g_{0}}
, for any
g_{0}
. There exists a field
\mathcal{K}
(§
3.7
) over which the base cycle
\mathfrak{z}_{g_{0}}
is defined.
Set
{R}:=\mathds{Z}[q_{v}^{-1},n_{E,F}^{-1}]
, where
n_{E,F}:=[E^{\times}\cap\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\mathfrak{O}_{1}^{\times}:F^{\times}]
.

Theorem 1.3.2

(Horizontal relations)
.

There exists a collection of cycles
\xi_{\mathfrak{f}}\subset{R}[\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})]
(for all
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{N}_{sp}
) each defined over
\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{f})
(constructed in §
4.6.1
) such that for every place
v\in\mathcal{P}_{sc}
, with
\mathfrak{p}_{v}\nmid\mathfrak{f}
, we have

H_{{w}}(\Fr_{{w}})\cdot\xi_{\mathfrak{f}}=\Tr_{\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{p}_{v}\mathfrak{f})/\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{f})}\xi_{\mathfrak{p}_{v}\mathfrak{f}},

where,
H_{{w}}
is the Hecke polynomial attached to
\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
at the place
{w}
of the reflex field
E=E(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
defined by
\iota_{v}
.

Remark 1.3.3
.

Actually, the cycles
\xi_{\mathfrak{f}}
for
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathcal{N}_{sp}^{r}
can be constructed in
\mathds{Z}[\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})]
if
r\geq 1
. For this it suffices to use
P_{\mu^{\varpi}}\in\mathcal{H}(\mathbf{G}(F)\sslash K,\mathds{Z})][X]
the minimal polynomial of
\mathcal{U}_{\mu^{\varpi}}
instead of
H_{{w}}
and exactly the same proof holds.

Remark 1.3.4
.

The methods used in the sections
4.5
to prove Theorem
1.3.2
for split places gives also, mutatis mutandis, horizontal norm-compatible systems for inert places of
F
.

1.4.
Arithmetic applications via split Kolyvagin systems

Kolyvagin’s original argument (see
[
Kol90
,
Kol91a
,
Kol91b
,
Kol91c
,
Gro91
]
as well as
[
How04
]
) uses the tame norm relations at inert auxiliary primes.
In all these variants of the same fundamental argument, one uses the fact that the global cohomology
H^{1}(\mathcal{K},\overline{T})
(resp., the local cohomology groups
H^{1}(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda},\overline{T})
)
decompose into two eigenspaces
H^{1}(\mathcal{K},\overline{T})^{\pm}
(resp.,
H^{1}(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda},\overline{T})^{\pm}
) for the action of a complex conjugation on the residual representation
\overline{T}
.

Instead of applying global duality for the entire cohomology
H^{1}(\mathcal{K},\overline{T})
, one does that for each of the eigenspaces
H^{1}(\mathcal{K},\overline{T})^{\pm}

and crucially uses the fact that if
\lambda
is a Kolyvagin prime then
H^{1}_{ur}(\mathcal{K}_{\lambda},\overline{T})^{\pm}
(being isomorphic to
(\overline{T}/(\Fr_{\lambda}-1)\overline{T})^{\pm}
) are both one-dimensional. In the case when
the residual representation
\overline{T}
of
G_{\mathcal{K}}
need not extend to a representation of
G_{F}
, one can no longer
apply the duality for the
\pm
-parts of the corresponding Selmer groups. If one attempts to use inert special primes
\lambda
, the local condition at
\lambda
will no longer be one, but higher-dimensional and the same argument with global duality will no longer work.

Very recently, Jetchev, Nekovář and Skinner
[
JNS18
]
have managed to solve this problem by using split instead of inert
auxiliary primes. In this case, if
w
and
\overline{w}
are the two places of
\mathcal{K}
above a split place
v
of
F
, one applies the duality simultaneously for
w
and
\overline{w}
where the local term in the duality becomes

H^{1}(\mathcal{K}_{v},\overline{T})/H^{1}_{ur}(\mathcal{K}_{v},\overline{T})\oplus H^{1}(\mathcal{K}_{\overline{v}},\overline{T})/H^{1}_{ur}(\mathcal{K}_{\overline{v}},\overline{T}).

By using a suitable application of the Čebotarev density theorem, Jetchev, Nekovář and Skinner still manage to run the argument, this time avoiding completely the action of complex conjugation on the residual Galois representation and the corresponding Selmer groups. The Kolyvagin systems obtained in this manner are referred to as split Kolyvagin systems.

This application of the methods of Jetchev, Nekovář and Skinner to the Gan–Gross–Prasad setting above, relies in a key way on the tame norm relations at split primes of Theorem
1.3.2
. This allows the construction of a split Kolyvagin system for cohomological Galois representations appearing in the middle-degree cohomology for Shimura varieties associated to certain product unitary isometry groups
\mathbf{U}(V)\times\mathbf{U}(W)
that appear naturally in the context of the Gan–Gross–Prasad conjectures.

1.5.

This paper consists of chapters VI and VII of the author’s EPFL 2019 thesis.
Therefore, I am thankful to my adviser D. Jetchev.
I am indebted and very grateful to C. Cornut and J. Nekovar for their meticulous and careful reading.
Finally, I thank Y. Dadoun for valuable discussions and comments.

2.
Unitary Shimura varieties

Notations

We fix an algebraic closure
\overline{\mathds{Q}}
of
\mathds{Q}
and an embedding
i_{\overline{\mathds{Q}}}\colon\overline{\mathds{Q}}\hookrightarrow\mathds{C}
. Let
F\subset\overline{\mathds{Q}}
be a totally real number field. Let
E
be an imaginary quadratic extension of
F
,
E
is usually said a CM field. Set
[E:\mathds{Q}]=2[F:\mathds{Q}]=2d
and let

\Sigma_{F}:=\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Q}}(F\,,\overline{\mathds{Q}})=\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Q}}(F\,,\mathds{R})=\{{\,\iota}_{i}\colon F\to\mathds{R},1\leq i\leq d\}

be the set of real embedding corresponding to the archimedean places of
F
. Let
\tau\colon x\mapsto x^{\tau}
be the non-trivial element of
\text{Gal}(E/F)
.

For each place
v
of
F
(possibly archimedean), we fix an algebraic closure
\overline{F}_{v}
of
F_{v}
and consider the set
\Sigma_{E,v}:=\text{Hom}_{F}(E\,,\overline{F}_{v})
. The Galois group
\text{Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/F_{v})
acts on
\Sigma_{E,v}
by post-composition: an element
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/F_{v})
sends any homomorphism
\varphi\in\Sigma_{E,v}
to
\sigma\circ\varphi\in\Sigma_{E,v}
. This yields a bijection

\Sigma_{E,v}/\text{Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/F_{v})\simeq\{w\text{ a place of }E\colon w\mid v\}

between
\text{Gal}(\overline{F}_{v}/F_{v})
-orbits of
F
-embeddings of
E
in
\overline{F}_{v}
and the places of
E
above
v
.
Set

{\Sigma}_{E}:=\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Q}}(E\,,\overline{\mathds{Q}})=\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Q}}(E\,,\mathds{C})=\bigsqcup_{\iota\in\Sigma_{F}}\Sigma_{E,\iota}.

For each
1\leq i\leq d
, choose an embedding
\widetilde{\iota}_{i}\in\Sigma_{E}
that extends
{\,\iota}_{i}\in\Sigma_{F}
. The set
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{E}=\{\widetilde{\iota}_{i}\colon 1\leq i\leq d\}
is a CM type for
E
, because
{\Sigma}_{E}:=\widetilde{\Sigma}_{E}\sqcup\widetilde{\Sigma}_{E}^{\tau}
. We denote by
c\colon x\mapsto\overline{x}
the complex conjugation of
\text{Gal}(\mathds{C}/\mathds{R})
, we then have
c\circ\widetilde{\iota}_{i}=\widetilde{\iota}_{i}\circ\tau
.

We fix, for each finite place
v
of
F
, an embedding
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{v}\colon\overline{F}}
F
_v.
We may then view elements of
\Sigma_{E}
(in particular
\Sigma_{E,\iota}
) as
v
-adic embeddings of
E
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma}_{E}}
Σ_E,v
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\iota}}

~

ι

_v:=
ι
_v ∘

~

ι

.

We fix a place of
E
above each finite place of
F
as follows: if
v
splits in
E
, we fix the place
w_{v}
to be the one defined by
\iota_{v}
and by abuse of notation, denote the other place by
\overline{w}_{v}
. If
v
is inert/ramified in
E
, we abuse notation and denote also by
v
the unique place of
E
above it.

2.1.
The groups

Fix a positive integer
n>1
and let
(V,\psi)
be a non-degenerate hermitian
E
-space of dimension
n+1
. Suppose that
8
8

8

For every
F
-algebra
R
, set
E_{R}:=E\otimes_{F}R
and
V_{R}:=V\otimes_{F}R=V\otimes_{E}E_{R}
. We define the action of
\tau
on
E_{R}=E\otimes_{F}R
by letting it act on left component. Then, extend
\psi
to a Hermitian form
\psi_{R}\colon V_{R}\times V_{R}\to E_{R}
as follows

\psi_{R}(v\otimes x,v^{\prime}\otimes y)=\psi_{R}(v,v^{\prime})xy^{\tau},\quad\forall v,v^{\prime}\in V\text{ and }\forall x,y\in E_{R}.

For example: For each
1\leq i\leq d
, the fixed embedding
{\,\iota}_{i}\colon F\to F_{v}
induces a natural
F
-algebra structure on
\mathds{R}
. Now letting
R=\mathds{R}
, one gets
(V,\psi)\otimes_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{i}}\mathds{R}:=(V_{R},\psi_{R})
, the hermitian space
(V\otimes_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{i}}\mathds{R},\psi_{i})
over
E\otimes_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{i}}\mathds{R}
.

\text{sign}((V,\psi)\otimes_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{i}}\mathds{R})=\begin{cases}(n,1)&\text{ if }i=1,\\
(n+1,0)&\text{ if }i\neq 1.\end{cases}

We consider the
F
-algebraic reductive group of unitary isometries
\mathbf{U}(V,\psi)
, this is a connected reductive group whose
R
-points, for any
F
-algebra
R
, are given by

\mathbf{U}(V,\psi)(R)=\{g\in\GL(V\otimes_{F}R)\colon\psi(gx,gy)=\psi(x,y),\forall x,y\in V\otimes_{F}R\}.

We will be mainly interested in the cases where
R=F_{v}
, the completion of
F
at finite places
v
, or
R=\mathds{A}_{F,f}
, the ring of finite adeles of
F
. Since our hermitian form
\psi
is fixed, we shall refer to the group of unitary isometries
\mathbf{U}(V,\psi)
as
\mathbf{U}(V)
.

Let
v\in V
be an anisotropic vector, every other
v^{\prime}\in V
such that
\psi(v^{\prime},v^{\prime})=\psi(v,v)
is conjugate under
\mathbf{SU}(V)(F)=\mathbf{U}(V)(F)\cap\SL(V)(E)
to
v
, (see
[
Shi08
, 1.5]
). Fix once and for all an anisotropic vector
w_{n+1}\in V
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\psi(w_{n+1},w_{n+1})=1

9
9

9

If
\psi(v,v)\neq 0
, one can choose any non-zero vector
v^{\prime}\in E\cdot v
and consider the modified hermitian
E
-space
(V,\psi(v,v)^{-1}\psi)
. But, we may have changed the signature. A better argument: By density of
V
in
V_{\iota_{1}}
, there is a vector
v\in V
with
\psi(v,v)
positive at
\iota_{1}
, hence everywhere. We choose this
v
and consider the hermitian
E
-space
(V,\psi(v,v)^{-1}\psi)
. Although the hermitian
E
-spaces are different, the associated unitary groups are isomorphic.
.
Set
D=E\cdot w_{n+1}
and
W=D^{\perp}
. Hence, the signature of
(D,\psi|_{D})
is
(1,0)
at all archimedean places of
F
. Consequently, the induced hermitian subspace
(W,\psi|_{W})
has signature
(n-1,1)
at the distinguished archimedean place
{\,\iota}_{1}
and
(n,0)
at the other archimedean places
{\,\iota}_{i},\forall 2\leq i\leq n
. Similarly, we associate to
(W,\psi|_{W})
the
F
-algebraic group of unitary isometries
\mathbf{U}(W)
.

Set
\mathbf{G}_{V}:=\Res_{F/\mathds{Q}}\mathbf{U}(V)
and
\mathbf{G}_{W}:=\Res_{F/\mathds{Q}}\mathbf{U}(W)
. Thus, for
\star\in\{V,W\}
and for any
\mathds{R}
-algebra
R
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{R}}(R)=\mathbf{U}(\star)(F\otimes_{\mathds{Q}}R)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}(\star)(F\otimes_{\mathds{Q}}\mathds{R}\otimes_{\mathds{R}}R)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{U}(\star)(\bigoplus_{\iota\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathds{R}_{\iota}\otimes_{\mathds{R}}R)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\iota\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathbf{U}(\star)(R_{\iota_{i}}),

where
\mathds{R}_{\iota}
is just
\mathds{R}
indexed by elements of
\Sigma_{F}
and
R_{\iota}
is
R
endowed with the
F
-algebra structure given by the embedding
\iota\colon F\hookrightarrow\mathds{R}
.
This yields
\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{R}}=\prod_{\iota\in\Sigma_{F}}\mathbf{G}_{\star,{\,\iota}}
where,

\mathbf{G}_{\star,{\,\iota}}=\mathbf{U}((\star,\psi)\otimes_{F,{\,\iota}}\mathds{R})\simeq\begin{cases}\mathbf{U}(\dim_{E}\star-1,1)_{\mathds{R}}&\text{ if }\iota=\iota_{1},\\
\mathbf{U}(\dim_{E}\star,0)_{\mathds{R}}&\text{ if }\iota\neq\iota_{1}.\end{cases}

Likewise

\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{C}}=\prod_{\widetilde{\iota}\in\widetilde{\Sigma}_{E}}\mathbf{G}_{\star,\widetilde{\iota}}\quad\text{ where }\quad\mathbf{G}_{\star,\widetilde{\iota}}=\GL(\star\otimes_{E,\widetilde{\iota}}\mathds{C})\simeq\GL(\dim_{E}\star)_{\mathds{C}}.

By left-exactness of the Weil restriction we have an embedding of
\mathds{Q}
-algebraic groups
\mathbf{G}_{W}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}_{V}
that identifies
\mathbf{G}_{W}
with the subgroup of
\mathbf{G}_{V}
given by:

\{g\in\mathbf{G}_{V}(R)\subset\GL(V\otimes_{\mathds{Q}}R)\colon g\cdot x=x,\quad\forall x\in D\otimes_{\mathds{Q}}R\},

for any
\mathds{Q}
-algebras
R
. Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{G}_{V}\times\mathbf{G}_{W}
and
\mathbf{H}=\Delta(\mathbf{G}_{W})\subset\mathbf{G}
, where
\Delta
denotes the diagonal embedding
\Delta\colon\mathbf{G}_{W}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
.

2.2.
The Deligne torus and variant

•

The torus
\Res_{\mathds{C}/\mathds{R}}\mathds{G}_{m,\mathds{C}}
is called the Deligne torus and is usually denoted by
\mathds{S}
. we will also consider its norm one subgroup
\mathbf{U}(1):=\mathbf{U}_{\mathds{C}/\mathds{R}}(1)
. We get by base change to
\mathds{C}
a canonical isomorphism of
\mathds{C}
-tori

\mathds{S}_{\mathds{C}}\simeq\mathds{G}_{m,\mathds{C}}\times\mathds{G}_{m,\mathds{C}},

where the factors are ordered in the way that
\mathds{S}(\mathds{R})=\mathds{C}^{\times}\to\mathds{S}(\mathds{C})=\mathds{C}^{\times}\times{\mathds{C}}^{\times}
is the map
z\mapsto(z,\overline{z})
.

Define the cocharacter
\mu=\mu_{\mathds{S}}\colon\mathds{G}_{m,\mathds{C}}\to\mathds{S}_{\mathds{C}}
, given on
\mathds{C}
-points by:
\mathds{C}^{\times}\to\mathds{S}_{\mathds{C}}(\mathds{C})\simeq\mathds{C}^{\times}\times\mathds{C}^{\times}
,
z\mapsto(z,1)
, one may also consider
\mu^{c}=\overline{\mu}_{\mathds{S}}
(
c
for complex conjugation) given on
\mathds{C}
-points by
z\mapsto(1,{z})
.

•

For each
\iota\in\Sigma_{F}
, consider the extension
E_{\iota}:=E\otimes_{F,\iota}\mathds{R}/\mathds{R}
. We will use the notation
\iota\mathds{S}:=\Res_{E_{\iota}/\mathds{R}}\mathds{G}_{m,E_{\iota}}
and
\widetilde{\iota}\mathbf{U}(1):=\mathbf{U}_{E_{\iota}/\mathds{R}}(1)
. Likewise, a base change to
E_{\iota}
yields an isomorphism

\iota\mathds{S}_{E_{\iota}}\simeq\mathds{G}_{m,E_{\iota}}\times\mathds{G}_{m,E_{\iota}},

where the factors are again ordered in the way that
E_{\iota}^{\times}\to\iota\mathds{S}(E_{\iota})=E_{\iota}^{\times}\times E_{\iota}^{\times}
is the map
z\mapsto(z,z^{\tau})
. Define similarly the cocharacter
\mu_{\iota}=\mu_{\iota\mathds{S}}\colon\mathds{G}_{m,E_{\iota}}\to\iota\mathds{S}_{E_{\iota}}
, given on
E_{\iota}
-points by:
E_{\iota}^{\times}\to\iota\mathds{S}_{E_{\iota}}(E_{\iota})\simeq E_{\iota}^{\times}\times E_{\iota}^{\times}
,
z\mapsto(z,1)
, one may also consider
\mu^{\tau}=\overline{\mu}_{\iota\mathds{S}}
given on
\mathds{C}
-points by
z\mapsto(1,{z})
.

•

, induces an isomorphism of fields

2.3.
The Hermitian symmetric domains

Let
\mathfrak{B}_{V}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n+1})
be an orthogonal
E
-basis of
(V,\psi)
, here
w_{n+1}
is the
E
-generator fixed in §
2.1
of the
E
-line
D=W^{\perp}
. Thus,
\mathfrak{B}_{W}:=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{n})
is also an orthogonal
E
-basis of
(W,\psi)
. Recall that
E_{{\iota}_{1}}=E\otimes_{F,{\,\iota}_{1}}\mathds{R}(\simeq\widetilde{\iota}_{1}(E)\otimes_{F,{\,\iota}_{1}}\mathds{R}=\mathds{C})
and let
\mathfrak{B}_{V,1}=(w_{1,1},\cdots,w_{n+1,1})
be the orthogonal
E_{{\iota}_{1}}
-basis of
(V_{{\iota}_{1}}:=V\otimes_{F,{\,\iota}_{1}}\mathds{R},\psi_{1})
obtained from
\mathfrak{B}_{V}
by base change along the distinguished
{\,\iota}_{1}\colon F\hookrightarrow\mathds{R}
. Since the signature of
(V_{\iota_{1}},\psi_{1})
is
(n,1)
, we may and will assume that
c_{1}=\psi_{1}(w_{1,1},w_{1,1})<0
and
c_{i}=\psi_{1}(w_{i,1},w_{i,1})>0
for all
1<i\leq n+1
. Consider for simplicity the orthogonal basis
\mathfrak{B}_{V,1}^{\prime}=\left(w_{1,1}^{\prime},w_{2,1}^{\prime}\cdots,w_{n+1,1}^{\prime}\right)=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{-c_{1}}}w_{1,1},\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{2}}}w_{2,1}\cdots,\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{n+1}}}w_{n+1,1}\right)
, thus the Hermitian form
\psi_{1}
with respect to
\mathfrak{B}_{V,1}^{\prime}
is given by

\psi_{1}(x,y)=-x_{1}{y}_{1}^{\tau}+\cdots+x_{n}{y}_{n}^{\tau}+x_{n+1}{y}_{n+1}^{\tau},

where
x=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}x_{i}w_{i,1}^{\prime}
and
y=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}y_{i}w_{i,1}^{\prime}
are two elements of
V_{{\iota}_{1}}
, with
x_{i},y_{i}\in E_{{\iota}_{1}}
. This form corresponds to the matrix
J_{\mathfrak{B}_{V}^{\prime}}=\diag(-1,1,\cdots,1)
. Likewise, let
J_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}^{\prime}}=\diag(-1,1,\cdots,1)
be the hermitian matrix corresponding to the basis
\mathfrak{B}_{W}^{\prime}=\left(w_{1,1}^{\prime},w_{2,1}^{\prime},\cdots,w_{n,1}^{\prime}\right)
.

Let us view the
E_{{\iota}_{1}}
-vectors space
V_{{\iota}_{1}}\simeq E_{{\iota}_{1}}^{n+1}(\simeq\mathds{C}^{n+1})
as being a union

V_{1}^{-}\cup V_{1}^{0}\cup V_{1}^{+},

of negative (resp. null, positive) vectors
x\in V_{{\iota}_{1}}
, depending on the sign of
\psi_{1}(x,x)\in\mathds{R}
. For instance

V_{1}^{-}=\left\{x=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}x_{i}w_{i,1}^{\prime}\in V_{{\iota}_{1}}\colon\psi_{1}(x,x)=-|x_{1}|^{2}+\sum_{i=2}^{{n+1}}|x_{i}|^{2}<0\right\}.

For every non-zero
x\in V_{{\iota}_{1}}
, all non-zero vectors of the ”complex”
10
10

10

By a slight abuse of language, we use the adjective complex here, since this is really a complex line up to base change along
\widetilde{\iota}_{1}
.
line
E_{{\iota}_{1}}x
have the same sign as
x
. Therefore, we can attach to each
E_{{\iota}_{1}}
-line in
V_{{\iota}_{1}}
a sign in
\{-,0,+\}
. In particular, we may think of
V_{1}^{-}
as the union of all negative lines in
V_{{\iota}_{1}}
. Let
\mathcal{X}_{V}
denote the set of negative lines in
V_{1}^{-}
.

Intersecting the negative lines with the hyperplane defined by

\left\{x=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}x_{i}w_{i,1}^{\prime}\in V_{{\iota}_{1}}\colon x_{1}=1\right\},

one gets an identification of
\mathcal{X}_{V}
with the complex open ball of dimension
n
:

\mathbb{B}_{n}:=\left\{x=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}x_{i}w_{i,1}^{\prime}\in V_{{\iota}_{1}}\colon x_{1}=1\text{ and }\sum_{i=2}^{{n+1}}|x_{i}|^{2}<1\right\}.

For instance, the ”complex” line
\ell_{V}=E_{\iota_{1}}w_{1,1}
is an element of
\mathcal{X}_{V}
. The line
\ell_{V}
corresponds to the centre
(1,0,\cdots,0)
(for the basis
\mathfrak{B}_{V}^{\prime}
) of the ball
\mathbb{B}_{n}
.

The group
\mathbf{G}_{V,{\,\iota}_{1}}(\mathds{R})\simeq\mathbf{U}(n,1)(\mathds{R})
acts transitively on the set
\mathcal{X}_{V}
and the stabilizer of
\ell_{V}
is isomorphic to
\mathbf{U}(n)\times\mathbf{U}(1)
(See
[
Gol99
, Lemma 3.1.3]
).

The negative line
\ell_{V}
determines a homomorphism of

2.6.
The Shimura varieties

Let us begin by proving some properties of the pairs
(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
and
(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})
:

Proposition 2.6.1
.

The pairs
(\mathbf{G}_{\star},\mathcal{X}_{\star})
,
\star\in\{V,W\}
are Shimura data (See
[
Mil17
, Definition 5.5]
), that is

SV1

The only characters of the induced representation
\text{Ad}\circ h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\colon\mathds{S}\to\GL(\text{Lie}(\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{C}}))
, are
z\mapsto z/\overline{z},1,\overline{z}/z
.

SV2

\text{Ad}\circ h
is a Cartan involution of
\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{R}}^{\der}
for all
h\in\mathcal{X}_{\star}
.

SV3

\mathbf{G}_{\star}^{\ad}
does not have any direct
\mathds{Q}
-factor
\mathbf{L}
on which
h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}
is trivial.

Proof.

For
(\mathbf{G}_{\star},\mathcal{X}_{\star})
,
\star\in\{V,W\}
:

SV1

Recall that we have defined the
\mathds{R}
-algebraic homomorphisms
h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\colon\mathds{S}\to\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{R}}\simeq\mathbf{U}(\dim_{E}\star-1,1)_{\mathds{R}}\times\mathbf{U}(\dim_{E}\star)_{\mathds{R}}^{d-1}
, given by

h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\colon\mathds{S}(\mathds{R})=\mathds{C}^{\times}\ni z\mapsto\left(\begin{pmatrix}z/\overline{z}&\\
&1_{\dim_{E}\star-1}\end{pmatrix},1_{\dim_{E}\star},\cdots,1_{\dim_{E}\star}\right)

with respect to the basis
\mathfrak{B}_{\star}
. It is then, straightforward to see that the only characters of the induced representation
\text{Ad}\circ h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\colon\mathds{S}\to\GL(\text{Lie}(\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{C}}))
, are
z\mapsto z/\overline{z},1,\overline{z}/z
.

SV2

The ss that
\text{Ad}\circ h
is a Cartan involution of
\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{R}}^{\der}
for all
h\in\mathcal{X}_{\star}
. Since, all Cartan involutions are conjugate by an inner automorphism, we just need to verify this axiom for
h=h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}
, that is to show that the following Lie group

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\mathbf{G}_{\star,\mathds{R}}^{\ad(h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i))}(\mathds{R})

\displaystyle:=\{g\in\SU(\star)(\mathds{C})\colon gh_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)=h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)\overline{g}\}

\displaystyle=\{g\in\SU(\star)_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{1}}(\mathds{C})\colon gh_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)=h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)\overline{g}\}\times\prod_{i=2}^{i=d}\SU(\star)_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{i}}(\mathds{C}),

\displaystyle\simeq\{g\in\SL(\star_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{1}}\otimes\mathds{C})\colon gJ_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}{}^{t}\overline{g}=J_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}},gh_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)=h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)\overline{g}\}\times\SU(\dim_{E}\star)(\mathds{C})^{d-1}

is compact. Indeed, for all

\{g\in\SL(\star_{F\,,{\,\iota}_{1}}\otimes\mathds{C})\colon gJ_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}{}^{t}\overline{g}=J_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\text{ and }gh_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)=h_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}(i)\overline{g}\}

Proposition 2.6.3
.

The Shimura variety
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
(resp.

) has the following decomposition

\bigsqcup_{g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{G}}}\Gamma_{g}\backslash\mathcal{X}\quad(\text{resp.}\quad\bigsqcup_{h\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{H}}}\Gamma_{h}\backslash\mathcal{Y}),

where for
{\textbf{X}}\in\{\mathbf{G},\mathbf{H}\}
, we have used
\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{X}}
to denote the finite class group
{\textbf{X}}(\mathds{Q})\backslash{\textbf{X}}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\textbf{X}}
and for
x\in\mathcal{C}_{\textbf{X}}
we put
\Gamma_{x}:=xK_{\textbf{X}}x^{-1}\cap{\textbf{X}}(\mathds{Q})\subset{\textbf{X}}(\mathds{R})
.

Proof.

This is an application of
[

, Lemma 5.13.]
.
∎

The subgroups
\Gamma_{x}
for
x\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{G}}
(resp.
x\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{H}}
) are congruence
19
19

19

We have an embedding
\pi\colon\mathbf{G}\hookrightarrow\GL(V\oplus W)\simeq\GL_{2n+1}
. A congruence subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})
is a subgroup containing a finite index subgroup of the form

\Gamma(N)=\pi(\mathbf{G})(\mathds{Q})\cap\{g\in\GL_{2n+1}(\mathds{Z})\colon g\equiv I_{2n+1}\mod N\},

for some integer
N
. The subgroup
\Gamma\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})
is arithmetic if
\pi(\Gamma)
is commensurable with
\pi(\mathbf{G})(\mathds{Q})\cap\GL_{2n+1}(\mathds{Z})
. Arithmetic and congruence subgroups of
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})
, are defined similarly.
arithmetic subgroups of
\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})
(resp.
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})
) (See
[
Mil17
, Proposition 4.1]
).

By the work of Baily and Borel
[
BB66
]
, each connected component
\Gamma_{g}\backslash\mathcal{X}
, (
{g\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{G}}}
) (resp.
\Gamma_{h}\backslash\mathcal{Y}
, (
{h\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{H}}}
)) can be endowed with a natural structure of a complex quasi-projective variety, hence also
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
and
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})
. Moreover, if
\Gamma_{x}
(for
x
in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{G}}
or
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{H}}
) is small enough (for example, if it is torsion-free) then
\Gamma_{g}\backslash\mathcal{X}
is smooth and its algebraic structure is unique. In this paper, we will be considering a stronger condition on the compact open subgroups
K_{\mathbf{G}}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
and
K_{\mathbf{H}}\subset\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, namely being neat
[
Pin89
, §0.1]
, it prevents
\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})
(resp.
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})
) from having fixed point in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K
(resp. (
\mathcal{Y}\times\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{H}}
)), in which case
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
and
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})
are smooth.

We have a better understanding of the algebraic structure governing the Shimura varieties
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
and
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})
. Indeed, It follows from results of Deligne, Borovoi, Milne and Moonen
[
Del71
,
Del79
,
Bor84
,
Mil83
,
Mil99
,
Moo98b
]
, that every Shimura varietiy has a canonical model
20
20

20

This canonical model depends only on the associated Shimura data. In particular, canonical models do not depend on the chosen open compact.
defined over its reflex field.

From now on, we will reserve the notation
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
and
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})
for the corresponding canonical model over the reflex field
E(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})=E(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})=E
. Therefore, the initial definition given in terms of double cosets gives actually the complex points of these models:

\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C})=\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})\backslash(\mathcal{X}\times(\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\penalty K_{\mathbf{G}})),\quad\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})(\mathds{C})=\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\backslash(\mathcal{Y}\times(\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\penalty K_{\mathbf{H}})).

2.7.
The projective system and Hecke action

For any two neat compact open subgroups
K^{\prime}\subset K
, we have an obvious quotient map

\pi_{K^{\prime},K}\colon\Sh_{K^{\prime}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C})\to\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C}),

which defines a finite étale morphism between
\Sh_{K^{\prime}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})\to\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
. Taking the projective system over neat compact subgroups,

\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})=\varprojlim_{K\text{ neat}}\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X}),

we obtain a a scheme over
\mathds{C}
, endowed with a continuous action of
\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
(see
[
Del79
, 2.1.4 and 2.7]
and
[
Mil90
, II.2 and II.10]
). Define also the quotient map
\pi_{\mathbf{G},K}\colon\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})\longrightarrow\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})/K=\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
.
The action of
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
denoted
T_{g}\colon\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})\to\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
on the system
(\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X}))_{K\text{ neat}}
defines an isomorphism of algebraic varieties between
\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
and
\Sh_{g^{-1}Kg}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
(See
[
Mil17
, Remark 5.29 & Theorem 13.6]
).
For a fixed finite level structure
K\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, The action of
T_{g}
on
\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
descends to a correspondence
\mathcal{T}_{g}\subset\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})\times_{E}\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
given by the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.0pt\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{G},K}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{G},K}}

The correspondence
\mathcal{T}_{g}=(\pi_{\mathbf{G},K})_{*}\cdot T_{g}\cdot(\pi_{\mathbf{G},K})^{*}
is finite, i.e.

\mathcal{T}_{g}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{fin}}(\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C})\times_{E}\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C})).

It is called the Hecke correspondence and is usually given by the diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh_{K_{g}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
Sh_K_g^-1(
G
,
X
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
Sh_K(
G
,
X
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{K_{g},K}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\times g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\pi_{K_{g^{-1}},K}}}

where, we have used the notation
K_{g}=K\cap gKg^{-1}
for (any)
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
.

2.8.
Deligne’s Reciprocity law for tori

2.8.1.
Artin map

Let
L
be any number field and set
\mathbf{X}_{L}:=\Res_{L/\mathds{Q}}\mathds{G}_{m,L}
. Class field theory states the existence of the so called Artin map, that is a continuous surjective homomorphism

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Art_{L}\colon\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})}
Gal
(L^ab/L),

sending uniformizers to geometric Frobenius elements.
Let
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
denotes the identity component for the real points and
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{+}=\ker(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\to\pi_{0}(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})))
. The kernel of
\Art_{L}
is precisely the closure
21
21

21

Here, the subgroups
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
is identified with the subgroup of
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})
of idele elements
\mathbf{z}=(z_{v})
in
\mathds{A}_{L}^{\times}
such that
z_{v}=1
if the place
v
is finite and
z_{v}>0
if the place
v
is real.
of
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
, see
[
NSW08
, §2 Chap. VIII]
.
The cited theorem affirms that the closure of the above product in the ideal class group is the kernel. Indeed the quotient map
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})/\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})
is open and continuous, thus the preimage of the closure of
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}\subset\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})/\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})
is the image of the closure of
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}\subset\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})
. Recall that by definition of
\Art_{L}
, we have
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})
is in its kernel and clearly
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
is also in the kernel, therefore since the homomorphism
\Art_{L}
is continuous and the target group
\text{Gal}(L^{ab}/L)
is Hausdorff the kernel must be closed and hence contains
\left(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}\right)^{-}\subset\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})
and this shows the equality.

By the real approximation theorem (See
[
Mil17
, Appendix, p. 152]
),
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})
is dense in
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})

thus one has a surjective map
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\twoheadrightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R}))
, thus
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})/\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}\simeq\pi_{0}(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R}))\simeq\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})/\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{+}\simeq\prod_{v\in\text{Hom}_{\mathds{Q}}(L,\mathds{R})}\{\pm\}
.

Lemma 2.8.1
.

The closure
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{-}\subset\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})
is equal to
L^{\times}(\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times})^{-}
.

Proof.

Let
x=(x_{v})\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{-}\subset\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})
and consider
(x_{n}=(x_{v,n})_{v})_{n\in\mathds{N}})\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})
a sequence that converges to
x
. There exists an open neighbourhood of
x
of the form
\mathcal{O}_{x}=\mathcal{O}_{x}^{S}\times\prod_{v\not\in S}\mathcal{O}_{L_{v}}^{\times}
for some open subgroup
\mathcal{O}_{x}^{S}\subset L^{S,\times}
such that for
n\geq N=N(\mathcal{O}_{x})
one has
x_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{x}
and
\ell_{x}:=\prod_{v\in S}x_{v}
lies in
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})=L^{\times}
. For each
v\in S
,
x_{v,n}
being convergent to
x_{v}
, then
|x_{v,n}|_{v}=|x_{v}|_{v}
is constant for
n
greater than some integer
N_{v}
that we choose to be greater than
N
. Put for
n\geq N_{x}:=\max_{v\in S}N_{v}
,

y_{n}:=\ell_{x}^{-1}x_{v,n}=(\frac{1}{x_{v}}x_{v,n})_{v\in S}\otimes(x_{v,n})_{v\not\in S}\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{L}^{\times}\cap L^{\times}=\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times}.

Then, one has

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\ell_{x}\lim_{n\to\infty}y_{n}\in L^{\times}(\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times})^{-}.\qed

Consider the homomorphism

\Art_{L,f}\colon\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})\to\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})/(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+})^{-},z\mapsto[(1_{\infty},z)].

Let us show that it is surjective. Using the density of
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})
in
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})
, observe that:

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}=\{(x,\Delta y)\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})\times\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})\colon

\displaystyle x\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R}),y\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(x)=\pi_{0}(y)\in\pi_{0}(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R}))\}.(\star)

Let
z=(z_{\infty},z_{f})\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})
, we may modify
z
by an element in
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
to kill
z_{\infty}
.
For this, consider
z^{\prime}=(z_{\infty}^{-1},\Delta z_{f}^{\prime})
where
z_{f}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})
is any element such that
\pi_{0}(z_{f}^{\prime})=\pi_{0}(z_{\infty}^{\prime-1})
(such element exists by density of the
\mathds{Q}
-points in the
\mathds{R}
-points of
\mathbf{X}_{L}
).
Hence,

z\mod\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}=zz^{\prime}\mod\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+},

and
zz^{\prime}\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})
. Accordingly

[z]\in\text{Im}(\Art_{L,f}),

which shows the surjectivity of
\Art_{L,f}
. Let us show that the kernel

\ker\Art_{L,f}=\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})\cap(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+})^{-}=(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{+})^{-},

where, the super-script
-
denotes the closure in
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})
first, then in
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})
.
Let
z\in(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})\cap\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+})^{-}
,
one then has (using
\star
) a sequence of
(x_{n},\Delta y_{n})\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
converging to
z
, so in particular
\lim_{n\to\infty}x_{n}=1
. Hence, there is an integer
N
big enough such that for
n\geq N
one has
\pi_{0}(x_{n})=\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
,
and accordingly
\pi_{0}(y_{n})=\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{R})^{+}
, i.e.
y_{n}\in\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{+}
.
Therefore,

z=\lim_{n\to\infty}(x_{n},y_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}(1_{\infty},y_{n})

which shows that
z\in(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{+})^{-}
where the closure is taken in
\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})
.
This shows that
\ker\Art_{L,f}\subset(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{+})^{-}
. The other inclusion is obvious.

In summary, this shows that

\displaystyle\text{Gal}(L^{ab}/L)

\displaystyle\simeq\pi_{0}(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})/\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q}))\simeq\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})/(\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{Q})^{+})^{-}.

By the above discussion we get an exact sequence

(2.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
(
X
_L(
Q
)^+)^-
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{X}_{L}(\mathds{A}_{f})}
Gal
(L^ab/L)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{L}}

It follows from the description of the connected components of Idèle class group in
[
AT90
, §9, Theorem 3]
that the kernel of
r_{L}
is isomorphic, as
\text{Aut}(L/\mathds{Q})
-module, to

L^{\times,+}\otimes(\mathds{A}_{f}/\mathds{Q})\simeq L^{\times,+}(\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times,+}\otimes(\mathds{A}_{f}/\mathds{Q}))\simeq L^{\times,+}(\mathcal{O}_{L}^{\times,+}\otimes(\widehat{\mathds{Z}}/\mathds{Z}))

yielding the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

_L^×,+⊗(

_f/

)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathds{A}_{L,f}^{\times}/L^{\times,+}}

2.8.2.
The cases

and

Now let us consider our two fields
E
and
F
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Art_{E}\colon\mathbf{T}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})}
Gal
(E^ab/E),
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Art_{F}\colon\Zbf(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})}
Gal
(F^ab/F).

The kernel of
\Art_{E}
(resp.
\Art_{F}
) is the closure of
\mathbf{T}(\mathds{R})^{+}\mathbf{T}(\mathds{Q})=\mathbf{T}(\mathds{R})\mathbf{T}(\mathds{Q})\simeq E^{\times}(\mathds{C}^{\times})^{d}
(resp.
\Zbf(\mathds{R})^{+}\Zbf(\mathds{Q})\simeq(\mathds{R}_{>0}^{\times})^{d}F^{\times}\simeq(\mathds{R}^{\times})^{d}(F^{\times})^{+}

\displaystyle\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathds{A}_{E,f}^{\times}/E^{\times}(\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times})^{-},

(\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times})^{-}
denotes the closure of
\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{O}_{E}}^{\times}
. Likewise,

\displaystyle\text{Gal}(F^{ab}/F)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/F^{\times,+}(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}.

2.8.3.
Deligne’s reciprocity law for
\mathbf{T}^{1}

Consider the ”zero”-dimensional Shimura datum
(\mathbf{T}^{1},\{\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\})
. Using the reflex norm map computed in §
2.5

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu\colon\mathbf{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{T}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\longmapsto\frac{z}{\overline{z}}}

we obtain the following reciprocity map

r=r(\mathbf{T}^{1},\{\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\})\colon\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)\longrightarrow\pi_{0}(\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})/\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q}))\simeq\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})^{-},

by composing the inverse of
\Art_{E}\colon E^{\times}(\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times})^{-}\backslash\mathds{A}_{E}^{\times}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)
and
\nu
. But, since
\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})
is discrete and hence closed in
\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})
(See remark
2.6.2
), the target of the map
r_{\text{fin}}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\{\det\mu_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}}\})
is actually
\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})
.

Let
K_{\mathbf{T}^{1}}\subset\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})
be a open compact subgroup. The action of
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)
on

[\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}},t]\in\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{T}^{1}}}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}})(\mathds{C})=\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})\backslash(\{\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\}\times\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{T}^{1}})

is defined by

\sigma([\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}},t]):=[{r_{\infty}}(\sigma)\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}},r_{\text{fin}}(\sigma)\,t].

Here, we have
{r_{\infty}}(\sigma)\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}=\det\mu_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}
and the map
r
factors then through its finite part
r_{\text{fin}}=r_{\text{fin}}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\{\det\mu_{\mathfrak{B}_{\star}}\})\colon\mathds{A}_{E}^{\times}\longrightarrow\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})
.

2.9.
Galois action on connected components

In this section we describe the action of
\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)
on the connected component of
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})
, for a fixed open compact subgroup
K_{\mathbf{H}}
of
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
. The derived group
\mathbf{G}^{\text{der}}
is simply connected
22
22

22

Indeed,
\mathbf{G}^{\text{der}}_{\overline{\mathds{Q}}}
is isomorphic to
\SL_{n,\overline{\mathds{Q}}}
.
. Therefore, one has a simplified description of the connected components of the Shimura variety:

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y}))

\displaystyle=\pi_{0}\left(\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\backslash(\mathcal{Y}\times(\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{H}}))\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{H}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{H}}\,\mathbf{H}^{\der}(\mathds{A}_{f})

\displaystyle\simeq{\mathbf{T}^{1}}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\det(K_{\mathbf{H}})

\displaystyle=\Sh_{\det(K_{\mathbf{H}})}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}})(\mathds{C})

The previous equalities uses the connectedness of
\mathcal{Y}
first, then density of
\mathbf{H}^{\der}(\mathds{Q})
in
\mathbf{H}^{\der}(\mathds{A}_{f})
(See
[
Mil17
, §5 - The structure of a Shimura variety]
).

Using the action of Galois group
\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)
on the complex points of
\Sh_{det(K_{\mathbf{H}})}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}})
as described in §
2.8
, we obtain that the action of
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)
on,

[\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}},t]\in\pi_{0}(\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y}))\simeq\Sh_{\det(K_{\mathbf{H}})}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\{\det\mu_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}}\})(\mathds{C}),

is given by

\sigma\left([\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}},t]\right)=[\det{\mu}_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}},r_{\text{fin}}(\sigma)\,t]
.

3.
Preliminary results on special cycles

3.1.
The set of special cycles
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})

The inclusion homomorphism
\mathbf{H}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}
, induces a map
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{R})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{G}(\mathds{R})
that sends the Hermitian symmetric domain
\mathcal{Y}
into
\mathcal{X}
. Therefore, we get a morphism of Shimura data
\varphi\colon(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
in the sense of
[
Mil17
, Definition 5.15]
.

Remark 3.1.1
.

By
[
Del71
, Theorem 1.15]
, the injective morphism of Shimura data
\varphi\colon(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
induces a closed immersion of Shimura varieties
\Sh(\varphi)\colon\Sh(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
meaning: For every ”sufficiently” small compact open subgroup
K_{\mathbf{G}}\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, there is a compact open subgroup
K_{\mathbf{H}}\subset\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
such that the map

{\Sh(\varphi)_{K_{\mathbf{H}},K_{\mathbf{G}}}}\colon\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X}),

given naturally on
\mathds{C}
-points by
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})(y,hK_{\mathbf{H}})\mapsto\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})(y,hK_{\mathbf{G}})
for any
y\in\mathcal{Y}
and
h\in\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, is a closed immersion. We then get the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 0.0pt\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{H},K_{\mathbf{H}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{G},K_{\mathbf{G}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\varphi)_{K_{\mathbf{H}},K_{\mathbf{G}}}}

To ease the notation we will omit the subscripts referring to the compacts in the map
\Sh(\varphi)_{K_{\mathbf{H}},K_{\mathbf{G}}}
.

For the remaining of this section, we will fix a neat compact open subgroup of
K\subset\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
.

Definition 3.1.2

(Special cycles)
.

We call a closed subvariety
\mathcal{Z}\subset\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
a
\mathbf{H}
-special cycle, if there exists an element
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
such that
\mathcal{Z}
is an irreducible component of the image of the map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})}
Sh(
G
,
X
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})}
Sh_K(
G
,
X
).

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathbf{G},K}}

Lemma 3.1.3
.

A closed subvariety
\mathcal{Z}\subset\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
is a
\mathbf{H}
-Special cycle if and only if there exists an element
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, such that

\mathcal{Z}=[\mathcal{Y}\times gK]\subset\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C}).

Proof.

The equivalence above follows immediately, using Remark
3.1.1
and by observing that

\mathcal{Z}=T_{g}\circ\Sh(\varphi)(\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})(K_{\mathbf{H},g}\times\mathcal{Y})),

where
K_{\mathbf{H},g}:=\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})\cap gKg^{-1}
, see
[
Moo98a
, Remark 2.6]
.
∎

For every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, we will denote by
\mathfrak{z}_{g}
the
{n}
-codimensional
\mathbf{H}
-special cycle
[\mathcal{Y}\times gK]\subset\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})(\mathds{C})
, as defined in Definition
3.1.2
. Set,

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H}):=\{\mathfrak{z}_{g}:g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})\}

Lemma 3.1.4
.

The natural map
\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})\to\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})
, induces the bijection

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})\simeq\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q}){Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K,

where,
{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}\simeq\mathbf{T}^{1}\times\mathbf{T}^{1}
denotes the center of
\mathbf{G}
.

Proof.

Using the total geodesicity of
\mathcal{Y}
and the Baire’s category theorem, one proves that

\mathcal{Z}_{K}\simeq\text{Stab}_{\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})}(\mathcal{Y})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\penalty{K}.

Then, one shows that
\text{Stab}_{\mathbf{G}(\mathds{Q})}(\mathcal{Y})=\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q}){Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})=N_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{H})(\mathds{Q})
. For more details, see
[
Jet16
, Lemma 2.3]
.
∎

Lemma 3.1.5
.

In the following bijection

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})\simeq\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K,

we can replace
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathbf{H})(\mathds{Q})
by its closure in
\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
:

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})=\left(\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\right)^{-}\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K=\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}}(\mathds{A}_{f})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K.

Proof.

The second
23
23

23

The first equality uses the fact that if
G
is a topological group (might even be locally compact) and
K
an open compact subgroup (being open implies the discreteness of the quotient
G/K
), then for every subgroup
H
of
G
we have
H\backslash G/K=\overline{H}\backslash G/K
, where
\overline{H}
is the closure of
H
in
G
.
equality is due to the fact that the closure of
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})
is
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}}(\mathds{A}_{f})
.
The latter fact is
[
Del79
, corollaire 2.0.9]
, because
\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}}
verifies the strong approximation for
\{\infty\}
since by assumption
\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}}
is simply connected, semisimple by definition and of noncompact type. Observe also, that
\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})^{-}=\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})
, since it is a copy of
\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})
, which is discrete (Remark
2.6.2
) and thus closed in
\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})
. ∎

3.2.
Fields of definition of cycles in
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})

Lemma 3.2.1
.

For every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, the cycle
\mathfrak{z}_{g}
is the image of the connected component

\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\cap K_{g,\mathbf{H}}\backslash\mathcal{Y}\in\pi_{0}(\Sh_{K_{g,\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})).

Proof.

For obvious topological reasons,
K_{g,\mathbf{H}}
is a compact open subgroup of
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
. The pre-image of
\mathfrak{z}_{g}
is the component over
[(1,1)]
:

\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\cap K_{g,\mathbf{H}}\backslash\mathcal{Y}\simeq\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H},g}^{\text{der}}}^{\circ}(\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}},\mathcal{Y}).

Here,
K_{\mathbf{H},g}^{\text{der}}\subset\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}}(\mathds{A}_{f})
is some open compact subgroup containing
K_{\mathbf{H},g}\cap\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}}(\mathds{A}_{f})
.
∎

Recall that, for any neat compact subgroup
K_{\mathbf{H}}\subset\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, we have

\pi_{0}(\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y}))\simeq{\mathbf{T}^{1}}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\det(K_{\mathbf{H}}).

The set of classes
\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{T}_{\mathbf{H}}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{T}^{1}}
of
\mathbf{T}^{1}
with respect to any compact open subgroup
K_{\mathbf{T}^{1}}\subset\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})
form an
Abelian group
. Therefore, the connected components of
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})
are all defined over abelian extensions of
E
. More precisely the field of definition
E_{g}
of the component
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H}}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})^{+}
over
[(1,1)]\in\Sh_{\det(K_{\mathbf{H}})}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\{\det\mu_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}}\})
is the finite abelian extension of
E
fixed by

\Art_{E}(r^{-1}(\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{R})\det(K_{\mathbf{H}}))),

where
r=r(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})\colon\mathds{A}_{E}\longrightarrow\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{\mathds{Q}})
is the reciprocity map constructed in §
2.8
and §
2.9
.

But since, for every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, the induced morphism
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H},g}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})\to\Sh_{K}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
is defined over
E

[
Mil17
, remark 13.8]
and every cycle
\mathfrak{z}_{g}
is then defined over the subfield
E_{g}
of
E^{ab}
that satisfies (the map
r
factors through its finite part):

\text{Gal}(E_{g}/E)=\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})\det(K_{\mathbf{H},g}).

3.3.
Transfer fields and reciprocity law

In this section, we will describe a bit further the field of definition of cycles
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})
, by computing the kernel of the reciprocity map constructed in §
2.8
a map

r_{\text{fin}}\colon\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E)\to\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})^{-}.

3.3.1.
The kernel of the Verlagerung map

The inclusion
F\hookrightarrow E
induces a commutative diagram

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Zbf(\mathds{A}_{f})}
T
(
A
_f)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Gal}(F^{ab}/F)}
Gal
(E^ab/E),

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{F/E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Art}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Art}_{E}}
Ver

where,
Ver
is the group-theoretic transfer map also called the Verlagerung map, see
[
Neu86
, p. 26]
.

Lemma 3.3.1
.

The kernel of the Verlagerung map is

\ker(\text{Ver}\colon\text{Gal}(F^{ab}/F)\to\text{Gal}(E^{ab}/E))\simeq F^{\times}/F^{\times,+}\simeq(\mathds{Z}/2\mathds{Z})^{d}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\ker(\text{Art}_{E})\cap\text{im}(i_{E/F})

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times})^{-}E^{\times}\cap i_{E/F}(\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times})

\displaystyle=(i_{E/F}(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+}))^{-}E^{\times}\cap i_{E/F}(\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times})

\displaystyle{=}i_{E/F}\left((\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}F^{\times}\cap\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\right)

\displaystyle=i_{E/F}\left((\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}F^{\times}\right)

where, we have used
E\cap i_{E/F}(\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times,+})=i_{E/F}({F}^{\times})
for (1) and then Dirichlet’s unit theorem; since
{[\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times}:\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}]}<\infty
, let
t_{1},\cdots,t_{?}
be a set of coset representatives for
\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times}/\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times}
, then

E^{\times}(\mathcal{O}_{E}^{\times})^{-}=E^{\times}(\cup_{i}t_{i}\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})^{-}=E^{\times}(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times})^{-}=E^{\times}(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}.

By commutativity of the above diagram (and injectivity of
i_{E/F}
) we get

\displaystyle\ker(\text{Ver})

\displaystyle=\text{Art}_{F}(i_{F/E}^{-1}(\ker(\text{Art}_{E})\cap\text{im}(i_{E/F})))

\displaystyle=\text{Art}_{F}((\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}F^{\times}).

Thus,

\ker(\text{Ver})\simeq(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}F^{\times}/\ker(\text{Art}_{F})=(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}F^{\times}/(\mathcal{O}_{F}^{\times,+})^{-}F^{\times,+}.

Accordingly, (See footnote
32
page
32
)

\ker(\text{Ver})\simeq F^{\times}/F^{\times,+}.

Finally, we get
F^{\times}/F^{\times,+}\simeq(\mathds{Z}/2\mathds{Z})^{d}
via the archimedean signature map:

\text{sgn}_{\infty}\colon F^{\times}\to F_{\mathds{R}}^{\times}/(F_{\mathds{R}}^{\times})^{2}\simeq\prod_{\iota\in\Sigma_{F}}\{\pm 1\},\quad z\mapsto(\iota(z)/|\iota(z_{\iota})|)_{\iota\in\Sigma_{F}},

which is surjective with kernel
F^{\times,+}
.∎

3.3.3.
Transfer fields of definition

In the remainder of this section, we focus on the relation between
E(\infty)
and ring class fields. For every
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-order
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathcal{O}_{E}
, we denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\times}
the group of units of its profinite completion. By extending
1\in\mathcal{O}_{E}
to a
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-basis we see that such order necessarily of the form
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{F}+\mathfrak{c}\mathcal{O}_{E}
where
\mathfrak{c}\subset\mathcal{O}_{F}
is a non-zero ideal of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
and

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}=({\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}\otimes\widehat{\mathds{Z}})^{\times}=(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{F}+\mathfrak{c}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{E})^{\times}\subset\mathds{A}_{E,f}^{\times}=(E\otimes\widehat{\mathds{Z}})^{\times}.

Definition 3.3.3
.

We attach to each
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathcal{O}_{F}+\mathfrak{c}\mathcal{O}_{E}
two subfields
E(\mathfrak{c})\subset E[\mathfrak{c}]\subset E^{ab}
:

(1)

The
ring class field of conductor
\mathfrak{c}
denoted
E[\mathfrak{c}]
, is the fixed field of
\Art_{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}E^{\times}/E^{\times})
, i.e.

\text{Gal}(E[\mathfrak{c}]/E)\simeq E^{\times}\backslash\mathds{A}_{E,f}^{\times}/\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}\simeq\Pic(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{c}}).

(2)

The
transfer field of conductor
\mathfrak{c}
denoted
E(\mathfrak{c})
, is the field whose norm subgroup is
E^{\times}\cdot\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}
, i.e.

\text{Gal}(E({\mathfrak{c}})/E)\simeq E^{\times}\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\backslash\mathds{A}_{E,f}^{\times}/\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}.

We have,

\text{Gal}(E[\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{c}}]/E(\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{c}}))\simeq\frac{E^{\times}\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}}{E^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}}\simeq\frac{\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}}{\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\cap E^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}}.

which is a quotient of
\Pic(\mathcal{O}_{F})
.

Now, If
F
has class number one (e.g.
\mathds{Q}
), then for any
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-order
\mathcal{O}
we have
E[\mathcal{O}]=E(\mathcal{O})
. Moreover, (
3.4
) implies that
E(\infty)=E[\infty]
.
For
\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}
two non-zero ideals of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
, we have
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}\cdot\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}}^{\times}=\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{gcd}(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}^{\times}
and

E^{\times}\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{lcm}(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}^{\times}\subset E^{\times}\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}\cap E^{\times}\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times},\quad E^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\text{lcm}(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}^{\times}\subset E^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}\cap E^{\times}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}}^{\times}

thus,

(3.3)

E[\mathfrak{c}]\cap E[\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}]=E[{\text{gcd}(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}],\quad E(\mathfrak{c})\cap E(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})=E({\text{gcd}(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})})

and

E[\mathfrak{c}]E[\mathfrak{c}^{\prime}]\subset E[{\text{lcm}(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}],\quad E(\mathfrak{c})E(\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})\subset E({\text{lcm}(\mathfrak{c},\mathfrak{c}^{\prime})}).

Set
E[\infty]=\cup_{\mathcal{O}}E[\mathcal{O}]
and
E(\infty)=\cup_{\mathcal{O}}E(\mathcal{O})
, where the union is taken over all
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-orders of
\mathcal{O}_{E}
. There exists a descending chain of
\mathcal{O}_{F}
-orders
\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}\}_{i\geq 1}
such that

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}=\bigcap_{i}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}^{\times},E[\infty]=\bigcup_{i}E[\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}^{\times}]\text{ and }E(\infty)=\bigcup_{i}E(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}^{\times}).

Accordingly,
\Art_{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times})=\cap_{i}\Art_{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathfrak{c}_{i}}^{\times})
. From which one deduces that

E[\infty]=(E^{ab})^{\Art_{E}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times})}\text{ and }E(\infty)=(E^{ab})^{\Art_{E}(\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times})}.

In other words, the transfer field
E(\infty)
is the subfield of
E^{ab}
fixed by
\Ver(\text{Gal}(F^{ab}/F))
. The field
E[\infty]
is a finite Galois extension of
E(\infty)
, with

(3.4)

\text{Gal}(E[\infty]/E(\infty))\simeq F^{\times}\backslash\mathds{A}_{F,f}^{\times}/\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{F}^{\times}\simeq\Pic(\mathcal{O}_{F}).

Moreover, the extension
E(\infty)/F
is Galois and its Galois group
\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/F)
is the Galois dihedral extension:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
Gal
(E(∞)/E)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/F)}
Gal
(E/F)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

equipped with the canonical splitting given by the complex conjugation

\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/F)\simeq\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E)\rtimes\{1,c\}.

From now on, we will use the notation

\Art_{E}^{1}\colon\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})/\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E),

for the inverse image of
r_{\text{fin}}(\mathbf{T}^{1},\{\det\mu_{\mathfrak{B}_{W}}\})
. Proposition
3.3.2
has the following immediate consequence:

Corollary 3.3.4
.

For every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, the field of definition
E_{g}
of the cycle
\mathfrak{z}_{g}\in\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})
is contained in the transfer field
E(\infty)
.

Remark 3.3.5
.

We warn the reader, that there exists two notions of ”ring class fields” in the literature. Our use of this terminology follows the one used in
[
CV05
,
CV07
]
. The second terminology, which is used in
[
Zha01
,
Nek07
]
, is what we called ”transfer fields”.

3.4.
Galois action via
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})

Proposition 3.4.1
.

For every
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E)
, let
h_{\sigma}\in\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
be any element satisfying
\Art_{E}^{1}\left(\det(h_{\sigma})\cdot\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})\right)=\sigma|_{E(\infty)}
. For every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, we have

\sigma(\mathfrak{z}_{g})=\mathfrak{z}_{h_{\sigma}g}.

Proof.

Fix an element
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
. In §
2.9
, we saw the description of the action of
\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E)
on the set of connected components of
\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H},g}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})(\mathds{C})
:

\bigsqcup_{h\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{H},g}}\Gamma_{h}\backslash\mathcal{Y},

where
\mathcal{C}_{\mathbf{H},g}=\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{H},g}
and
\Gamma_{h}=\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\cap hK_{\mathbf{H},g}h^{-1}=\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\cap K_{\mathbf{H},hg}
.

This description says that, for every
\sigma\in\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E)
, if we let
h_{\sigma}\in\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
be any element verifying
\Art_{E}^{1}(\det(h_{\sigma})\cdot\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q}))=\sigma|_{E(\infty)}
, then

\left(\Gamma_{h}\backslash\mathcal{Y}\right)^{\sigma}=\Gamma_{h_{\sigma}h}\backslash\mathcal{Y}.

On the other hand by Lemma
3.2.1
, the cycle
\mathfrak{z}_{g}
is the image of
\Gamma_{1}\backslash\mathcal{Y}=K_{\mathbf{H},g}\backslash\mathcal{Y}
by the closed immersion
\Sh(\phi)_{K_{\mathbf{H}},K_{\mathbf{G}}}\colon\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{H},g}}(\mathbf{H},\mathcal{Y})\rightarrow\Sh_{K_{\mathbf{G}}}(\mathbf{G},\mathcal{X})
. Reccall that
\Sh(\phi)_{K_{\mathbf{H}},K_{\mathbf{G}}}
is defined over
E

[
Mil17
, remark 13.8]
. Therefore,
\sigma(\mathfrak{z}_{g})=\mathfrak{z}_{h_{\sigma}g}.

∎

Consequently, the left action of
\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})
on the set of
\mathbf{H}
-special cycles

\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})=\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}^{\der}(\mathds{A}_{f})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K_{\mathbf{G}},

descends to an action of

\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}^{\text{der}}(\mathds{A}_{f})\backslash\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})\simeq\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{Q})\backslash\mathbf{T}^{1}(\mathds{A}_{f})\simeq\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E),

which yields,

\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E)\backslash\mathcal{Z}_{\mathbf{G},K}(\mathbf{H})\simeq\mathbf{Z}_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})\backslash\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})/K.

Using the above proposition, we see that for every
g\in\mathbf{G}(\mathds{A}_{f})
, the cycle
\mathfrak{z}_{g}
is defined over the subfield
E_{g}^{\prime}\subset E(\infty)
, given by
25
25

25

The field
E_{g}^{\prime}
is contained in the field
E_{g}
defined at the end of §
3.2
.

\text{Gal}(E(\infty)/E_{g}^{\prime})=\text{Art}_{E}^{1}(\det\left((Z_{\mathbf{G}}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}(\mathds{Q})\mathbf{H}^{\der}(\mathds{A}_{f})gKg^{-1})\cap\mathbf{H}(\mathds{A}_{f})\right).
